The categorical basis of dynamical entropy
Suddhasattwa Das

Abstract
Many branches of theoretical and applied mathematics require a quantifiable notion of complexity. One such circumstance is a topological dynamical system - which involves a continuous self-map on a metric space. There are many notions of complexity one can assign to the repeated iterations of the map. One of the foundational discoveries of dynamical systems theory is that these have a common limit, known as the topological entropy of the system. We present a category-theoretic view of topological dynamical entropy, which reveals that the common limit is a consequence of the structural assumptions on these notions. One of the key tools developed is that of a qualifying pair of functors, which ensure a limit preserving property in a manner similar to the sandwiching theorem from Real Analysis. It is shown that the diameter and Lebesgue number of open covers of a compact space, form a qualifying pair of functors. The various notions of complexity are expressed as functors, and natural transformations between these functors lead to their joint convergence to the common limit. MSC 2020 classification : 18A25, 18B35, 18A30, 37B40, 7B02 Keywords : Topological entropy, open covers, comma category, Kan extension, preorder



1  Introduction

A bounded dynamical system on a metric space is described by the following simple set of assumptions :

Ground assumption.

There is a compact metric space
\Omega
of diameter
1
, and a continuous function
\mathcal{T}:\Omega\to\Omega
.

Here, we have assumed without any loss of generality that the metric on bounded space
\Omega
has been normalized to have diameter
1
. The focus of topological dynamical systems theory is to derive and describe properties of the system from these basic assumptions. The objects that are usually in consideration are various invariant behavior such as attractors, invariant sets and omega-limit sets, and various asymptotic properties such as invariant measures, stable and unstable manifolds, and entropy. Our focus is on a notion called entropy. More precisely, we are going to reprove a classical result on entropy using a categorical route. This would involve a categorical reformulation of several topological notions such as open covers, diameter and Lebesgue number. The categorical formulation brings to light various structural properties of the dynamics, i.e. various functors it induces.

The role of the dynamics map
f
is to redistribute the points in
\Omega
. Thus neighborhoods get scattered, entangled or mixed, and the metric also gets finer and less smooth. All these phenomenon reveal in their own way, the complexity of the dynamics.
Topological entropy or simply, entropy, is a measure of the rate of growth of complexity of a dynamical system. There are multiple notions of complexity one can ascribe to a dynamical system, and as a result, multiple definitions of entropy
(
Walters1975
;
Walters2000
;
PesinPitskel1984
;
Mummert2007
;
Climenhaga_entropy
;
ClimenhagaThompson2012
, e.g.)
. They are three main notions of entropy based on partitions, and changes in the metric. All of them are directly inspired from physical phenomena and experimental attempts to observe dynamics. See Figure
1
for a summary of these notions, and our how they are interpreted as functors.

Figure 1
:
Outline of the categorical approach. Section
1
describes the three classical ways of quantifying the growth of complexity of a dynamical system. We interpret each of these notions of complexity as functors
\dyn
(
10
),
\OrbitSep
(
15
) and
\OrbitSpan
(
15
). We have labelled these as the dynamics induced functors. We define a category of growth rates denoted
\mathcal{Z}
(
13
) and show that the three dynamics induced functors lead to entropy functors
\Pressure_{1}
(
14
) and
\Pressure_{2},\Pressure_{3}
(
16
). These are the functorial representation of the sequences described in Equation (
5
), which describe how the classical notions of complexity grow with successive iterations of the dynamical system. The asymptotic exponential growth rate of these sequences are interpretable as the colimit functor, and we derive a general categorical result (Theorem
7
) to show that they are equal.

Refinement of partitions

One can track the growth of complexity by tracking how open covers of
\Omega
get increasingly finer. This notion of complexity is related to the information theoretic complexity associated with data / measurements taken from the dynamics of
\left(\Omega,f\right)

(
grassberger1991inf
;
vallee1987inf
, e.g.)
, and has important consequences to computability
Spandl_sofic_2008
;
BDWY2020
. Suppose one intends to track the dynamics on the phase space
\Omega
by means of a computer. Being a finite state machine, the computer approximates
\Omega
via a discrete collection of sets whose union covers
\Omega
. To avoid boundary issues, we assume that these sets are open sets. Thus, the matter of observation of a space
\Omega
becomes naturally associated with the concept of an
open cover
.

Given an open cover
\alpha
of
\Omega
, any point
x
on
\Omega
receives an address-identity based on whichever pieces of
\alpha
it lies in. If
x
lies at the intersection of multiple pieces, this address is obviously non-unique. Moreover, different points in
\Omega
may be assigned the same address-identity. Recall that the
diameter
of a set is the maximum possible distance between any two points within it. If all the pieces of
\alpha
have a diameter less than
\epsilon
, then any address-identity of
x
specifies its location to within an error bound of
\epsilon
.

Dynamical systems is about the study of trajectories. Continuing the same observation scheme, the
n
-length orbit of
x
, which is the sequence
x,f(x),\ldots,f^{n-1}(x)
, is specified by a sequence of address locations. In other words, an orbit is to be described by the computer as a sequence
\alpha_{i_{0}},\ldots,\alpha_{i_{n-1}}
of pieces of
\alpha
, such that
f^{t}(x)\in\alpha_{i_{t}}
. Now note that given this sequence
\alpha_{i_{0}},\ldots,\alpha_{i_{n-1}}
, the location of the starting point
x
gets narrowed down to the set

\alpha\left(i_{0},\ldots,i_{n-1}\right):=\cap_{t=0}^{n-1}f^{-t}\alpha_{i_{t}}.

Thus with each iteration of the dynamical system, and each additional effort to locate the new position of the point
x
, one gets a progressively refined estimate of the initial location of
x
. In fact the set
\alpha\left(i_{0},\ldots,i_{n-1}\right)
above is a piece of the
dynamically refined
partition

\alpha^{(n)}=\vee_{t=0}^{n-1}f^{-t}\alpha=\left\{\alpha\left(i_{0},\ldots,i_{n-1}\right)\;:\;\mbox{all sequences }i_{0},\ldots,i_{n-1}\right\}.

But now note that for an arbitrary sequence
i_{0},\ldots,i_{n-1}
, the intersection

\alpha\left(i_{0},\ldots,i_{n-1}\right)
may be empty. Moreover, one may not need all of the pieces of
\alpha^{(n)}
to cover
\Omega
. From a computational point of view, one would need to retain a minimum number
\CovNum\left(\alpha^{(n)}\right)
of pieces. This number
\CovNum\left(\alpha^{(n)}\right)
may be interpreted as the minimum size of a subcover of
\alpha^{(n)}
. Note that
\CovNum\left(\alpha^{(n)}\right)
is finite by the compactness of
\Omega
.

In summary, any open cover
\alpha
(finite or not) produces a sequence of numbers

\CovNum\left(\alpha^{(1)}\right),\CovNum\left(\alpha^{(2)}\right),\ldots,\CovNum\left(\alpha^{(n)}\right),\ldots

The first notion of entropy studies the asymptotic exponential rate at which this sequence grows, as the size / diameter of the partition
\alpha
diminishes. The open cover
\alpha
can be interpreted to be a coarse grained partition of division of the space
\Omega
. Then
\CovNum\left(\alpha^{(n)}\right)
is the minimum storage required to use the alphabet / addresses of
\alpha
to describe all possible trajectories of length
n
.

Refinement of the metric

Instead of tracking the dynamics using a cover, one could release a finite collection of tracers or sensors into the phase space, and track their movements. Such tracers can be specified by their set of initial locations, a finite subset
A
of
\Omega
. Each
a\in A
has a trajectory
\{f^{t}(a)\}_{t=0,1,2,\ldots}
, and the objective is that an arbitrary trajectory
\{f^{t}(x)\}_{t=0,1,2,\ldots}
can be followed or closely approximated by at least one of the tracer trajectories. More precisely, for each
n
and finite set
A
, we are interested in

\sup_{x\in\Omega}\;\inf_{a\in A}\;\max_{0\leq t<n}\;d\left(f^{t}x,f^{t}a\right).

(1)

The dual to this problem of covering the phase spaces with tracers, is the task of finding independent trajectories. The independence of the points in a finite set
A
can be measured as the closest their trajectories get to each other, i.e., as the quantity

\inf_{a\neq a^{\prime}\in A}\;\max_{0\leq t<n}d\left(f^{t}a,f^{t}a^{\prime}\right).

(2)

The choice of such a set of independent points is crucial in applications such as finding
landmark points

(
LongFerguson2019
;
LiangPaisley2015
;
SilvaEtAl_landmark_2006
, e.g.)
for manifold learning. The min-max problems of (
1
) and (
2
) may be understood better from the realization that the starting metric
d
on
\Omega
is refined under repeated action of the dynamics. For each
n
, define

d_{n}(x,y)=\max_{0\leq t<n}d\left(f^{t}x,f^{t}y\right).

This leads to a series of metrics
d=d_{0}<<d_{1}<<d_{2}<<\ldots
, where
<<
indicates that each metric is successively finer. The metric
d_{n}
incorporates some information about the dynamics, by taking into account the future
n
states of each point. Given any metric
\tau
on
x
that generates its topology, and finite set
A\subset\Omega
we can define two quantities

\text{metric-sep}(A,\tau):=\min\left\{\tau(x,y)\;:\;x,y\in A,\,x\neq y\right\}.\quad\text{metric-span}(A,\tau):=\max\left\{\tau(x,A)\;:\;x\in X\right\}.

(3)

As
n
grows, the metric
d_{n}
grows finer. As a result, both the quantities
metric-sep
and
metric-span
increase. As a result,
metric-span
and
metric-sep
can reformulate the optimization problems in (
1
) and (
2
) as

\begin{split}H_{2}(\epsilon,n)&:=\min\left\{\left|A\right|\;:\;A\subset_{fin}\Omega,\;\text{metric-span}\left(A,d_{n}\right)>\epsilon\right\}.\\
H_{3}(\epsilon,n)&:=\max\left\{\left|A\right|\;:\;A\subset_{fin}\Omega,\;\text{metric-sep}\left(A,d_{n}\right)<\epsilon\right\}.\end{split}

(4)

Thus,
H_{2}(\epsilon,n)
is the smallest size of a discrete set
A
such that the
n
-orbit of every point is within
\epsilon
distance of the
n
-orbit of some point in
A
. Similarly,
H_{3}
covers a dual notion of how separated
n
-orbits remain.

Goal

The following well known result from ergodic theory
(e.g.
Walters2000
, Theorem 9.4)
shows that complexity may be measured in
4
different ways, and each have the same asymptotic limit, called the
topological entropy
of the dynamics :

Corollary 1

(Topological entropy)
.

For any continuous dynamical system on a bounded metric space,

\begin{split}&\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\diam(\alpha)\geq\epsilon}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\ln\CovNum\left(\alpha^{(n)}\right)=\sup_{\alpha}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\ln\CovNum\left(\alpha^{(n)}\right)\\
&=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\ln H_{2}(\epsilon,n)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\ln H_{3}(\epsilon,n).\end{split}

(5)

Our goal is to rediscover Corollary
1
by interpreting several notions of dynamics, entropy and limits using categorical means. All the limits in (
5
) will be interpreted as colimits of functors, as summarized in Figure
1
. Their equality will be established by revealing a web of relations between the functors, and is based on a purely categorical result we state and prove as Theorem
7
. An overview of these inter-relations is also presented in Figure
2
. These relations are based on an interpretation of some basic topological constructs, namely covers, diameter, and Lebesgue number. We show that these familiar notions can be reinterpreted as functors. We derive two other purely category theoretical results in Theorems
2
and
5
, which brings to light the structural role played by these concepts. Corollary
1
eventually turns out to be a consequence of these reinterpretations of topology and dynamics.

Previous work.

Due to the many facets of dynamical systems, there are different ways one can find categorical structure in dynamics. For example, one could characterize the dynamics via its limiting sets
calcines2013limit
, their state-space action
spivak2015steady
;
ngotiaoco2017
, collection of orbits
jaz2020double
. There has been notable investigations into entropy in the context of measure theory
(
Baez2014_bayesian
;
BaezEtAl2011_info
, e.g.)
separately. Most of the categorical treatment of dynamical systems
(
lomadze1999time
;
Delvenne2019_dyn
;
Suda2022Poincare
;
MossPerrone2022ergdc
, e.g.)
deal with the global structure of dynamical systems and do not pursue the inner, topological changes that take place. Our work provides a new joint perspective into dynamics and topology. An important consideration for us the existence and equalities of limits, which from a categorical point of view, translates into colimits. One of the key contributions is the notion of a
qualifying pair
of functors, defined and studied in Section
3
. We show how such a pair can be created simultaneously, and have important consequences to the existence of colimits. More importantly, they play a central role in the existence and uniqueness of the limits in Corollary
1
.

Outline

We begin our categorical approach by re-examining some basic concepts of topology in Section
2
. The most important outcome is that the notions of diameter and Lebesgue number of a partition are reinterpreted as functors, or more precisely, as
Kan extensions
. In Section
3
we introduce the notion of a qualifying pair. Diameter and Lebesgue number are shown to be such a pair. The existence of qualifying pairs have important consequences when studying limiting behavior, or more precisely, colimits. We examine this in detail in Section
4
. This completes the category theoretical background for the work. Next, we look at the functors that are induced by the dynamics in Section
5
. The notions of complexity introduced in (
4
) and (
5
) are given a functorial redefinition. Finally, Corollary
1
is proved in Section
6
. This is done by interlinking the complexity functors using the category theoretical tools we have developed. The proofs of the lemmas and Theorems that are used on the way, are provided in Section
7
.

Notation

Throughout the rest of the paper, we will use the notation
F:X\to Y
to denote a functor between categories
X,Y
. Given two functors
F,G:X\to Y
, the notation
F\Rightarrow G
signifies a natural transformation from
F
to
G
. Identity map. The set of objects of a category
\mathcal{C}
will be denoted as
ob\left(\mathcal{C}\right)
.

2  Building blocks of metric topology

One of the fundamental notions of topology is that of open covers. Open covers are a means of granularizing or discretizing a continuum. Any finite representation of a continuum, such as using a computer, relies on the creation of an open cover. The complexity of a dynamical system may be studied by means of such representation using open covers. We shall now discover that the set of open covers
\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
form a preorder, i.e., a category in which there is at most one morphism between any two objects.

Given a preorder, a morphism
f:a\to b
between two objects can be unambiguously denoted by an arrow
a\to b
. Thus the morphisms together create a partial ordering of the objects. There are two ways one can assign a partial ordering to
\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
. For any two open covers
a,b
:

1.

a\to b
if for every set
S_{b}
in
b
, there is a set
S_{a}
in
a
such that
S_{a}\supseteq S_{b}
.

2.

a\to b
if for every set
S_{a}
in
a
, there is a set
S_{b}
in
b
such that
S_{a}\supseteq S_{b}
.

These preorder structures on the collection of open covers are different and provide different interpretations of the notion - “
a
is coarser than
b
” via the morphism
a\to b
. We choose the first option. We next consider a more trivial subclass of open covers : the collection
\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket
of covers all of whose pieces are open disks of a constant radius. We call this the subcategory of
uniform disk covers
.

There are a few other fundamental preorder categories which are tied to the study of
\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
. The first is
\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket
- the preorder of numbers in
[0,\infty]
ordered by the
\leq
relation. Its opposite category is
\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket
- the preorder of numbers in
[0,\infty]
ordered by the
\geq
relation. One can similarly define
\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket
and
\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\downarrow\right\rrbracket
, the subcategories of
\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket
and
\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket
respectively, generated by the integers. Finally, we define
\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket
to be the subcategory of
\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket
obtained by restriction to the set
(0,1]
. It is useful to model any process of
parameterization
by a bounded, non-negative number
\epsilon
as
\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket
, if one is interested in the limiting behaviour as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
.

The categories
\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket
,
\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
and
\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket
are linked together by some functors fundamental to metric topology. First, there is an inclusion map
\iota_{UDC}:\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
as an inclusion of subcategories. Next, recall that a functor between two preorders is the same as an order preserving map between the respective set of objects. As a result, the map which assigns every uniform disk cover in
\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket
its constant radius, is also a functor

\Grain:\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket

The
\Grain
functor might seem trivial, but it is the key to building the more universal idea of diameter. Next, one can assign to each
\epsilon\in ob\left(\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket\right)
the open cover containing all possible disks of radius
\epsilon
. This is the free UDC generated by
\epsilon
. One in fact can represent this as a functor

\Free:\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket.

The categories
\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket
and the functors
\Grain,\Free
will be the building blocks of topological entropy. The study of open covers and open sets involve the use of several quantifiable notions such as diameter and Lebesgue number. The advantage of the categorical point of view is that these notions can be derived as structural properties. We next describe
Kan extensions
, one of the key tools to derive new structural properties from an existing arrangement of categories and functors.

Kan extensions

Kan extensions
(
perrone2022kan
;
street2004categorical
;
Riehl_homotopy_2014
, e.g.)
are universal constructions which generalize the practice of taking partial minima or maxima, in a functorial manner. Suppose we have two functors
E\xleftarrow{F}X\xrightarrow{K}D
. Although not necessary to the definition, one can interpret
F
as a measurement on a parameter space
X
, and
K:X\to D
to be a projection to a subset of coordinates. A
left Kan extension
of
F
along
K
is a functor
\mathcal{L}_{F}\left(K\right):D\to E
along with a minimum natural transformation
\eta:F\Rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{F}\left(K\right)\circ K
. Moreover, this pair
\left(\mathcal{L}_{F}\left(K\right),\eta\right)
is minimum / universal in the sense that for every other functor
H:D\to E
along with a natural transformation
\gamma:F\Rightarrow H\circ K
, there is a natural transformation
\tilde{\gamma}:\mathcal{L}_{F}\left(K\right)\Rightarrow H
s.t.
\gamma=\left(\tilde{\gamma}\star\Id_{K}\right)\circ\eta
. This is shown in the diagram below.

\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{L}_{F}\left(K\right),\quad\hbox to179.99pt{\vbox to81.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 89.99356pt\lower-41.82285pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

One can similarly define a
right Kan extension
of
F
along
K
. It is a functor
\mathcal{R}_{F}\left(K\right):D\to E
along with a natural transformation
\epsilon:\mathcal{R}_{F}\left(K\right)\circ K\Rightarrow F
. Moreover, this pair
\left(\mathcal{R}_{F}\left(K\right),\epsilon\right)
is maximum / universal in the sense that for every other functor
H:D\to E
along with a natural transformation
\gamma:H\circ K\Rightarrow F
, there is a natural transformation
\tilde{\gamma}:H\Rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{H}\left(K\right)
such that
\gamma=\epsilon\circ\left(\tilde{K}\star\Id_{K}\right)
. This is shown in the diagram below.

\mathcal{R}:=\mathcal{R}_{F}\left(K\right),\quad\hbox to179.99pt{\vbox to90.59pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 89.99356pt\lower-41.82285pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

If
E
is a co-complete category, the left Kan extension always exists. Similarly if
E
is a complete category, the right Kan extension always exists. In case both left and right Kan extensions of
F
along
K
exist, they combine to produce the following diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{F}\left(K\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}_{F}\left(K\right)}

(6)

We prove in Lemma
2.1
that
\Grain,\Free
satisfy the following assumptions :

Assumption 1
.

There is a embedding
\iota_{X}:\tilde{X}\to X
of categories.

Assumption 2
.

There are functors
G:\tilde{X}\to\mathcal{E}
and
G^{r}:\mathcal{E}\to\tilde{X}
such that
GG^{r}=\Id_{\mathcal{E}}
and
G^{r}G\Rightarrow\Id_{X}
.

Assumption 3
.

The category
\mathcal{E}
is complete, and the right Kan extension
\mathcal{R}_{\iota\Free}\left(\Id_{\mathcal{E}}\right)
of
\Id_{\mathcal{E}}
along
\iota G^{r}
exists

Lemma 2.1
.

Assumptions
1
,
3
and
2
are satisfied if one takes
\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket,\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket,\Grain
and
\Free
to be
\tilde{X},X,\mathcal{E},G
and
G^{r}
respectively.

The totally ordered set
\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket
is clearly complete. Covers in
\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket
are clearly open covers and thus elements in
\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
. Thus the inclusion of
\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket
in
\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
is an embedded
\iota_{udc}
. Since all the categories involved are preorders, and the maps
\Grain
and
\Free
are order preserving,
\Grain
and
\Free
are also functors. To verify Assumption
3
, note that the completeness of
\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket
stems from the fact that its objects form the right closed interval
(0,1]
. The left Kan extension exists and is defined pointwise as

\mathcal{R}_{\iota\Free}\left(\Id_{\mathcal{E}}\right)(\alpha):=\sup\left\{\epsilon\in(0,1]\;:\;\alpha\to\Free(\epsilon\right\}.

Recall that the Lebesgue number of an open cover
\alpha
is the maximum number
\delta
such that for every
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
and any ball
B
of diameter
\delta^{\prime}
, there is a piece of
\alpha
which contains
B
. In fact Lebesgue number is a Kan extension, as stated below :

Lemma 2.2
.

The Lebesgue number and diameter of an open cover are right and left Kan extensions :

\LebNum:=\mathcal{R}_{\iota\Free}\left(\Id_{\mathcal{E}}\right):\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket,\quad\diam:=\mathcal{L}_{\iota_{UDC}}\left(\Grain\right):\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket.

Lemma
2.2
is proved in Section
7.1
. the constructions of
\LebNum
and
\diam
can be stated more abstractly using the notations of Assumptions
1
,
2
. Thus, one can similarly define the left and right Kan extensions

L:=\mathcal{R}_{\iota G^{r}}\left(\Id_{\mathcal{E}}\right):X\to\mathcal{E},\quad D:=\mathcal{L}_{\iota}\left(G\right):X\to\mathcal{E}.

(7)

The commutation between the various spaces are shown on the left part of (
8
). The right part relates this abstract framework to our concrete examples in metric topology.

(8)

So far we have presented the elementary notions of open covers and disk-open covers as categories
\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
and
\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket
, and then shown how the more sophisticated notions of diameter and Lebesgue number are consequences of the mere arrangements between
\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
and
\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket
. In fact, the technique of Kan extensions leads to a more significant property for the pair
\left(\diam,\LebNum\right)
. We discuss this next.

3  Qualifying pair of functors

The diameter and Lebesgue number functors can be arranged as
\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\LebNum}\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket\xleftarrow{\diam}\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
. Recall that given a general arrangement of functors and categories such as
D\xrightarrow{F}C\xleftarrow{G}E
, one can create a
comma category

(
polonsky2020local
, e.g.)

\left[F\;;\;G\right]
whose objects are

\left\{\left(d,e,\phi\right)\;:\;d\in ob(D),\,e\in ob(E),\,\phi\in\Hom_{C}\left(Fd;Ge\right)\right\}.

A morphism between two objects
(d,\phi,e)
and
(d^{\prime},\phi^{\prime},e^{\prime})
in this category are all those pairs of morphisms
\left\{(f,g)\;:\;f\in\Hom_{D}\left(d;d^{\prime}\right),\,g\in\Hom_{E}\left(e;e^{\prime}\right)\right\}
characterized by the following equation :

Comma categories thus store the commutation relations between
F
and
G
, when projected to
C
. Comma categories have a
forgetful functor

\Forget:\left[F\;;\;G\right]\to D\times E,

which forgets the commutation information stored within the comma category and projects onto the base category. The action of
\Forget
on objects and morphisms is described as

(d,\phi,e)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Forget}}{{\mapsto}}(d,e),\quad\hbox to59.51pt{\vbox to52.66pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 29.75691pt\lower-26.32838pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-29.75691pt}{-20.16866pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.35277pt}{-1.74107pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

In the special case when
D=E=C
and
F=G=\Id_{C}
, the resulting comma category
\left[\Id_{C}\;;\;\Id_{C}\right]
is known as the
arrow category

(
winter2009arrow
;
steingartner2014categorical
, e.g.)
of
C
. It is the category arising out of the commutation relations in
C
. Comma categories arise naturally in many applications of category theory

(
grandis1997categorically
;
burke2018synthetic
, e.g.)
, and even in the pointwise construction of Kan extensions. We use the language of comma and arrow categories to define a special property for a pair of functors within the same functor category.

Qualifying pairs

Let
C,D
be categories. A pair of functors
F,G:C\to D
is said to be a
qualifying pair
if
F\Rightarrow G
, and there is a functor
\Psi:\left[F\;;\;G\right]\to\left[\Id_{C}\;;\;\Id_{C}\right]
satisfying the following commutation of functors :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[F\;;\;G\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\times C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\Id_{C}\;;\;\Id_{C}\right]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}

An object
\left(c_{1},c_{2},\phi\right)
of
\left[F\;;\;G\right]
indicates the morphism
Fc_{1}\xrightarrow{\phi}Gc_{2}
in
D
. This object is mapped into
\left(c_{1},c_{2}\right)
under
\Forget
. The presence of the commuting map
\Psi
which is equivariant under
\Forget
means that
Fc_{1}\xrightarrow{\phi}Gc_{2}
is mapped into
c_{1}\xrightarrow{\Psi(\phi)}c_{2}
. Moreover, this mapping is functorial, meaning that morphisms are mapped into morphisms. This is illustrated in the diagram below, in which the red and blue arrows are corresponding objects in their respective comma categories.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}Fc_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}Gc_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Fc^{\prime}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Gc^{\prime}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c^{\prime}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c^{\prime}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\left(c_{1},c_{2}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left(c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{2}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ff_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gf_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi(\phi^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1},f_{2}}

The first main result shows that the basic Assumptions
1
,
2
guarantee the existence of a qualifying pair.

Theorem 2

(Qualifying pair construction)
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
2
hold, and assume the notation of (
7
). Then

1.

D
and
L
are left and right Kan extensions respectively, of
\Id_{\mathcal{E}}
along
\iota G^{r}
.

2.

D\Rightarrow L
.

3.

Now suppose that
\mathcal{E}
is totally ordered. Then
(L,D)
form a qualifying pair of functors.

Theorem
2
is proved in Section
7.2
. Theorem
2
(i) thus offers a definition for
D
and
L
which is an alternative to (
7
). Now they are revealed to be left and right Kan extensions respectively, of
\Id_{\mathcal{E}}
along
\iota G^{r}
. By virtue of Lemma
2.1
, the fundamental metric notions of diameter and Lebesgue number now appear to be left and right Kan extensions respectively along a trivial inclusion functor.

The next result reveals the rationale behind the name “qualifying pair”.

Theorem 3

(Qualifying action)
.

Let
I,C,D
be categories and
F,G:C\to D
be a qualifying pair of functors. Then for any pair of functors
F^{\prime}:I\to C
and
G^{\prime}:I\to C
, any natural transformation
\eta:FF^{\prime}\Rightarrow GG^{\prime}
is mapped into a natural transformation
\Psi_{*}\eta:F^{\prime}\Rightarrow G^{\prime}
.

Theorem
3
is proved in Section
7.3
. Thus for any pair of functors
F^{\prime},G^{\prime}
from the functor category
\text{Func}\left(I\;;\;C\right)
, the composite
FF^{\prime}
can be naturally transformed to
GG^{\prime}
only if
F^{\prime}
can be naturally transformed to
G^{\prime}
. By virtue of Lemma
2.1
and Theorem
2
, this can be interpreted in the context of metric topology as follows :

Corollary 4

(Diameter-Lebesgue num. pair)
.

The pair of functors
\left(\LebNum,\diam\right)
is a qualifying pair. Moreover, for any category
I
and functors
F^{\prime},G^{\prime}:I\to\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
,

\LebNum(F^{\prime}(i))\leq\diam(G^{\prime}(i)),\;\forall i\in ob(I)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad F^{\prime}(i)<G^{\prime}(i),\;\forall i\in ob(I).

Thus Lebesgue number of a cover
\alpha
being smaller than the diameter of another cover
\beta
“qualifies” the relation
\alpha\to\beta
, i.e.,
\beta
is a finer subcover of
\alpha
. Corollary
4
implicitly relies on the following elementary result :

Lemma 3.1
.

Given two functors
F,G:X\to Y
between preorders
X,Y
, there is a natural transformation
\eta:F\Rightarrow G
iff
G
is pointwise greater than
F
, i.e., for each
x\in ob(X)
,
F(x)\to G(x)
. In that case the natural transformation
\eta
is unique.

Lemma
3.1
says that any existence result on natural transformation between functors is simply a statement about such pointwise inequalities.

A reinterpretation

Recall that post-composition with
F
leads to a functor between functor categories

F\circ:C^{I}\to D^{I},\quad H\mapsto F\circ H.

Similarly, one has the functor
G\circ:C^{I}\to D^{I}
. These two functors
F\circ
and
G\circ
have the same domain and codomain, so one can construct their comma category
\left[F\circ\;;\;G\circ\right]
. Theorem
3
says that the qualifier
\Psi
of the pair
(F,G)
induces the map

\Psi_{*}:\left[F\circ\;;\;G\circ\right]\to\Hom_{C^{I}}

(9)

Equation (
9
) summarizes the primary role played by qualifying pairs. Note that the elements in
\Hom_{C^{I}}
are natural transformations between functors from
I
to
C
. This ability of qualifying pairs to produce natural transformations has important consequences in studying limiting behavior. We study this next.

4  Qualifying pair and (co)limits

Our final goal is a categorical understanding of entropy, and that involves interpreting many asymptotic dynamic properties as colimits. In this section we review how colimits change as functors are extended or adjointed.
We begin with a chain of functors
N\xrightarrow{P}X\xrightarrow{m}Z
between some categories
N,Z
. Then in general one would have

Lemma 4.1
.

For any sequence of functors
N\xrightarrow{P}X\xrightarrow{m}Z
, if the colimits
\colim m
and
\colim mP
exist, then there is a morphism
\colim mP\to\colim m
.

This has special significance if
Z
is a preorder, in which one can interpret the relation
\colim mP\to\colim P
as the colimit of
mP
being greater than the colimit of
P
. If
m:X\to Z
is interpreted as a measure, and
P:N\to X
a parameterized family in
X
, then according to Lemma
4.1
, the colimit of the measure on the parameterized family is greater than the colimit of the measure. However, if the parameterization
P
bears a certain spanning property, then the colimits would be equal. We now formally define this spanning via a generalization of inverses.

Pre- and post- right adjoints

Let
T:A\to B
be a functor. Then
T^{*}:B\to A
is said to be a
pre-right adjoint
to
T
if
TT^{*}\Rightarrow\Id_{B}
. Such a functor
T
is said to be
pre-right adjoint enabled
or in brief
pre-r.a.e.
.
Similarly,
T^{*}:B\to A
is said to be a
post-right adjoint
to
T
if
\Id_{B}\Rightarrow TT^{*}
. In that case
T
will be called
post-right adjoint enabled
or in brief
post-r.a.e.
.

Post-right adjoints generalize the notion of divergence to infinity of sequences. As an example, take
A=\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket
and
B=\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket
. Any functor
T:A\to B
corresponds to a non-decreasing sequence of non-negative numbers
0\leq x_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq\ldots
. Suppose

\bar{x}:=\colim T=\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}=\infty.

Now define a map
T^{*}:\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}\to\mathbb{N}
as

T^{*}(x):=\min\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}\;:\;x_{n}\geq x\right\}.

Since
T^{*}
is order preserving it is a functor
T^{*}:\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket
. Note that by construction, we have
TT^{*}\Rightarrow\Id_{\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket}
. Also, it was possible to define
T^{*}
only because the
x_{n}
s converge to infinity. Thus although
T:\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket
is an embedding, it
spans
the whole range of
\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket
. This idea of spanning is captured by a pre-right adjoint. The following lemma shows an important consequence of this spanning action.

Lemma 4.2
.

[Limit and colimit preservation] Let
R:B\to C
be a functor.

1.

Suppose
T:A\to B
has a pre-right adjoint. Then if the limits
\lim R
and
\lim RT
exist, then they are equal.

2.

Suppose
T:A\to B
has a post- right adjoint. Then if the colimits
\colim R
and
\colim RT
exist, then they are equal.

Lemma
4.2
is proved in Section
7.4
. We are now ready to state our main theorem.

Theorem 5

(Limits under qualifying pair)
.

Let
Z
be a co-complete category, and
(D,L)
is a qualifying pair of functors arranged in the following diagram of categories and functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(m\right)}

Then :

1.

There is a morphism
\colim mP\to\colim\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(m\right)
.

Henceforth we assume a functor
P:N\to X
such that
DP:N\to\mathcal{E}
has a post-right adjoint.

2.

the functor
P
has a post-right adjoint.

3.

\colim m=\colim mP
.

4.

\colim\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(m\right)\to\colim mP
.

5.

If
Z
is a preorder, then all the above morphisms are equalities.

Theorem
5
is proved in Sec
7.5
. Theorem
5
says that a parameterized limit of a measurement
m
would lead to the same limit as
m
, provided the parameterization “spans the end” of the space. This notion of spanning the end is captured in the requirement of having a post-right adjoint.
Theorems
2
and
5
lead to this immediate result :

Corollary 6

(Colimits along sequences)
.

Now suppose that
X
has finite coproducts,
\mathcal{E}
has countable coproducts, and
\left(x_{n}\right)_{n}
is a sequence of objects in
X
such that the sequence
D(x_{n})
is monotonic and has a post-right adjoint. Let
m:X\to Z
be a functor into a co-complete category
Z
. Then
\colim\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(m\right)\to\limsup_{n\to\infty}m(x_{n})
.

Corollary
6
is proved in Section
7.6
.
This completes the statement of all our results from general Category theory. We next investigate some functors that arise from the dynamical system.

5  Functors induced by the dynamics

We are now in a position to describe the categorical interpretation of classical entropy, as summarized in Figure
1
. We begin with the first definition, which is based on open covers. The category
\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
has finite coproducts. The category theoretic coproduct of two open covers corresponds to the operation of “join”, defined as

a\vee b:=\left\{S_{a}\cap S_{b}\;:\;S_{a}\in a,\,S_{b}\in b\right\}.

Since
\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
is also a preorder, a product of a finite collection of elements is equivalent to a minimum. Next note that the dynamics behaves as an endofunctor on
\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
. In general, coproducts and endofunctors combine to produce a
dynamics functor
:

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be any category with finite coproducts, and
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
be an endofunctor. Then this induces a functor
\dyn_{F}:\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
, whose action on objects is given by

\dyn_{F}(n,c):=\vee_{t=0}^{n-1}F^{t}c

The proof of Lemma
5.1
is direct and will be omitted. We call
\dyn_{F}
the dynamics functor associated to the endofunctor
F
. Now note that given any open cover
a\in\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
, one gets a new open cover

\mathcal{T}^{*}a:=\left\{\mathcal{T}^{-1}S_{a}\;:\;S_{a}\in a\right\}.

The fact that the elements in the collection
\mathcal{T}^{*}a
are open sets follows from the continuity of
\mathcal{T}
. Thus we get a functor

\mathcal{T}^{*}:\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket

and as a result, by Lemma
5.1
, the associated dynamics functor :

\dyn_{\mathcal{T}}:\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\times\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket,\quad(n,\alpha)\mapsto\alpha\wedge\mathcal{T}^{*}\alpha\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathcal{T}^{*(n-1)}\alpha.

(10)

For brevity, we shall denote
\dyn_{\mathcal{T}}
by
\dyn
. This is the point where we introduce the first notion of complexity. Consider the following map

\CovNum:\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket,\quad C\mapsto\mbox{ minimum size of a subcover of }C.

(11)

This map
\CovNum
clearly preserves the partial order on
\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
and is thus a functor too. Now consider the composite of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\times\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dyn}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CovNum}

This functor will be our first notion of complexity or
entropy
. We will find it more useful to rewrite
h
as

h:=\CovNum\circ\dyn:\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket^{\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket}

(12)

This is due to the natural bijection between functors
\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\times\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket
and
\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket^{\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket}
. The category
\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket^{\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket}
is of special importance to us, we call it the category of
rates
. For brevity we denote it as

\mathcal{Z}:=\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket^{\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket}.

(13)

The category
\mathcal{Z}
has as objects all non-decreasing sequences of non-negative numbers, ordered by pointwise
\leq
relations. The functors
h
and
\diam
fit together along with Kan extensions into the following commuting diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diam}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{h}\left(\diam\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}_{h}\left(\diam\right)}

The following left Kan extension will help capture the first two notions of complexity in (
5
) :

\Pressure_{1}:=\mathcal{L}_{h}\left(\diam\right):\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket\to\mathcal{Z}.

(14)

This completes an task of representing the notions of covering complexity as a functor.

Next we provide functorial characterizations of
H_{2},H_{3}
. It begins with the realization that the collection of finite subsets of
\Omega
form a preorder
\FinSet
, with a preorder structure provided by the inclusion relations. Thus given two finite subset
A,A^{\prime}
of
X
,
A\to A^{\prime}
iff
A\subseteq A^{\prime}
. As a result, cardinality is a functor :

\|\cdot\|:\FinSet\to\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket.

The quantities
metric-sep
and
metric-span
from (
3
) can now be stated as functors

\begin{split}\OrbitSpan:\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\times\FinSet\to\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket,&\quad(n,A)\mapsto\text{metric-span}\left(A,d_{n}\right)\\
\OrbitSep:\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\times\FinSet\to\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket,&\quad(n,A)\mapsto\text{metric-sep}(A,d_{n})\end{split}

(15)

The functors
\OrbitSpan
and
\OrbitSep
are functors naturally induced by the dynamics, along with the functor
h
from (
12
). Given a pair
(n,A)
in
\mathbb{N}\times\FinSet
, the notation
\tilde{\left\|(n,A)\right\|}
will be interpreted as the composite functor :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\times\FinSet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\FinSet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\|\cdot\|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\proj_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\|\cdot\|}

Next we interpret the operations of taking extrema along one variable in (
4
), into Kan extensions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\times\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\times\FinSet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\times\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pressure_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\|\cdot\|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id_{N}\times\OrbitSpan}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id_{N}\times\OrbitSep}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pressure_{3}}

These functors
\Pressure_{2}
and
\Pressure_{3}
reveal the connection of the analogous quantities from (
4
) with the topological structure of the dynamics. In summary :

\begin{split}\Pressure_{2}:=\mathcal{R}_{\left|\cdot\right|\circ\proj_{2}}\left(\Id_{N}\times\OrbitSpan\right),&\quad\Pressure_{2}(n,\epsilon):=\inf\left\{\left|A\right|\;:\;A\in\FinSet,\,\OrbitSpan(A,n)>\epsilon\right\}\\
\Pressure_{3}:=\mathcal{L}_{\left|\cdot\right|\circ\proj_{2}}\left(\Id_{N}\times\OrbitSep\right),&\quad\Pressure_{3}(n,\epsilon):=\sup\left\{\left|A\right|\;:\;A\in\FinSet,\,\epsilon>\OrbitSep(A,n)\right\}\end{split}

(16)

Finally, we recast the functors
\Pressure_{2},\Pressure_{3}:\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\times\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\uparrow_{0}^{\infty}\right\rrbracket
as

\Pressure_{2}:\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket\to\mathcal{Z},\quad\Pressure_{3}:\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket\to\mathcal{Z},

where
\mathcal{Z}
was defined in (
13
). This completes the task of expressing each of the notions of entropy as functors. In the next section we examine how they are inter-related via natural transformations. An overview of their inter-relations is presented in Figure
2
.

Figure 2
:
Entanglement network between entropy functors. The figure shows a graph on 7 vertices, with each vertex drawn as a box. For each
1\leq i\leq 7
, the
i
-th vertex represents a functor
F_{i}:C_{i}\to\mathcal{Z}
, from some category
C_{i}
into the same codomain
\mathcal{Z}
, which is the rate category (
13
). Each such functor represents a functorial / structural way of assigning a complexity sequence to a topological dynamical system
f:\Omega\to\Omega
. Each edge from a vertex
i
to a vertex
j
represents a sub-structural relationship between
F_{i}
and
F_{j}
, made precise in Theorem
7
. The existence of such an edge implies that the colimit of
F_{i}
is less than or equal to the colimit of
F_{j}
. Since this entangle network is strongly connected, all these colimits must be equal. These colimits are related to the classical entropy limits expressed in (
5
), and thus they are proved to be equal.

6  Network of diagrams

In this section we prove Corollary
1
using the category theoretical machinery we have been building. The following theorem is the key tool using which we interconnect the various functors developed so far.

Theorem 7

(Entanglement theorem)
.

Suppose
S
is a cocomplete category, and
\mathcal{G}
is a directed graph on n vertices. Moreover

1.

For each vertex
i
, there is a category
C_{i}
and a functor
F_{i}:C_{i}\to S
.

2.

For each edge
i\to j
, there is a category
A_{i,j}
, a functor
F_{i,j}:C_{i}\to C_{j}
with a post right adjoint, a functor
G_{i,j}:A_{i,j}\to C_{j}
and a natural transformation
\eta_{i,j}
as shown

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i,j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{i,j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{i,j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{i}}
\eta_{i,j}

(17)

Then for every
1\leq i,j\leq n
,

1.

If there is a path from i to j, then
\colim F_{i}\to\colim F_{j}
.

2.

In particular, if
S
is a preorder, then
i,j
are in the same connected component of
\mathcal{G}
iff
\colim F_{i}=\colim F_{j}
.

Theorem
7
is proved in Section
7.7
.

Remark

An analogous result can be stated for limits instead of colimits, in which the pre-right adjoint requirement has to replaced by an analogous pre-left adjoint condition.

Remark

The natural transformations
\eta_{i,j}
play the role of an
entanglement
. This entanglement is not between functors
F_{i}
and
F_{j}
, but between their extensions from a
pre-parameterization
category
A_{i,j}
.

Existence of post-right adjoints

When applying Theorem
7
to any arrangement of functors and categories, one would need to verify the condition that the functor
F_{i,j}
has a post-right adjoint. The following lemma provides a tool for verifying this.

Lemma 6.1

(Composition of post-r.a.e. functors)
.

The composition of two functors with post right adjoints, also has a post right adjoint.

Lemma
6.1
is proved in Section
7.8
. One key functor we shall rely on is defined the functorial version of the multiplication by
a
:

\times_{a}:\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket,\quad x\mapsto\begin{cases}ax&x\leq 1/a\\
1&x\geq 1/a\end{cases}.

(18)

It is a standard practice in calculus to rely on such scaling functions to preserve the study of limits as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
. This is formalized below.

Lemma 6.2
.

For every positive number
a>0
, the functor
\times_{a}:\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket
has a post right adjoint, which is division by
a
. Further, division by
a
is a right inverse to
\times_{a}
.

We shall use a combination of Lemmas
6.1
and
6.2
to interpret the relations between the various entropy functors in the light of Theorem
7
.

Structural relations

We list three natural transformations that are fundamental to the functors induced by the dynamics. These provide a precise meaning to the edge connections between the various complexity functors, as drawn in Figure
2
.

1.

The following commuting diagram is a functorial restatement of metric relations
(
Walters2000
, Sec 7.2, Rem 5)
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pressure_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pressure_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 0.5}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pressure_{2}}
\eta_{2,3}
\eta_{3,2}

(19)

The transformation
\eta_{3,2}
simply says that the counts provided by metric span is greater than the counts provided by metric separation. On the other hand,
\eta_{2,3}
reverses this inequality, if one halves the grain size
\epsilon
.

2.

The following commuting diagram is a functorial restatement of combinatorial relations
(
Walters2000
, Thm 7.7)
between covering complexity, and metric span and separation.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pressure_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 1.01}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diam}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\LebNum}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 0.49}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pressure_{2}}
\eta_{3,H}
\eta_{H,2}

(20)

3.

The final structural stems from purely topological properties. Now suppose
C
is an UDC of diameter / grain
\epsilon
. Consider the UDC
\tilde{C}
with disks having the same centers as those of
C
, but with radius
2.01\epsilon
. Now consider any point
x\in\Omega
. Then there must be a point
y
such that the disk
B(y,\epsilon)
of diameter
\epsilon
and center
y
lies in the cover
C
. Then note that by the triangle inequality, the disk
B(x,\epsilon)
must lie within the disk
B(y,2.1\epsilon)
. Therefore, the UDC
\tilde{C}
has a Lebesgue number at least
\epsilon
and is coarser than
C
. Thus we have a diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times_{2.01}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Grain}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Expand}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diam}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Leb}
\eta_{exp}

(21)

Here
\Expand
is the functorial expansion from
C
to
\tilde{C}
just described, and
\times_{2.01}:\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket
is defined as in (
18
). In fact since
\Grain=\diam\circ\iota_{UDC}
, we have from (
21
) :

\Leb\Expand\Rightarrow\diam\iota_{UDC},

which by the qualifying action of
\left(\diam,\Leb\right)
implies
\Expand\Rightarrow\Id_{UDC}
.

Exhaustive sequence of covers

In our application of Theorem
7
, in most cases our choice of
A_{i,j}
will be
\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket
. Note that we have at our disposal a functor
\Gamma:\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket
such that in the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diam\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{UDC}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diam}

(22)

the functor
\Gamma\diam
has a post-right adjoint. This is because one can find a nested sequence of UDC-s
\left\{\Gamma_{n}\right\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that their diameters monotonically converge to
0^{+}
. Set
\tilde{\Gamma}_{n}:=\Expand\left(\Gamma_{n}\right)
, the UDC of grain
2\diam\left(C_{n}\right)
.

We now combine the various functors and natural transformations from (
19
), (
20
), (
21
), (
22
) into the following
entanglement
diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{R}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{R}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{R}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\text{Cov}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{R}\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{R}\right]}
LogLim
LogLim
LogLim
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Gamma}}

Expand

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{UDC}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diam}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pressure_{1}:=h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 1.01}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 2.01}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pressure_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pressure_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{UDC}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diam}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\LebNum}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pressure_{1}:=h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 0.49}
LogLim

\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pressure_{3}}
\eta_{2,H}
\eta_{H,3}
\eta_{exp}
\eta_{3,2}

(23)

The functors shown in blue will play the role of the
\Pressure_{i}
in Theorem
7
. The natural transformations shown in red correspond to the entanglements
\eta_{i,j}
. The equality of limits in (
5
) involve limits of the form
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\ln\left(\cdot\right)
. This corresponds to the following functor

\LogRate:\mathcal{Z}\to\left[\mathbb{R}\right]^{\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket},\quad\left(a_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mapsto\left(\frac{1}{n}\ln a_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}.

Now the category
\left[\mathbb{R}\right]^{\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket}
has as objects all non-decreasing sequences of reals. The action of taking supremum of such sequences corresponds to the colimit functor. Concatenating, we get the following functor shown by a dashed arrow :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{R}\right]^{\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[\mathbb{R}\right]}
LogLim
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\LogRate}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\colim}

This functor
LogLim
has been appended to the entanglement diagram in (
23
), and indicated in orange.

Proof of Corollary
1

Consider an instance of Theorem
7
in which the graph has three nodes. The functors
\Pressure_{1},\Pressure_{2},\Pressure_{3}
have already been defined. See Table
1
for a list of the functor
C_{i}A_{i,j}\xleftarrow{F_{i,j}}\xrightarrow{G_{i,j}}C_{j}
, and the natural transformations
\eta_{i,j}
.

Table 1
:
Entanglements between various entropy functors. The entanglement is in terms of Theorem
7
. The various functors that form the components of this entanglement are given in the diagram in (
23
).

Edge
i\to j

A_{i,j}

F_{i,j}

G_{i,j}

\eta_{i,j}

2\to 1

\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket

\times_{1.01}\diam\iota_{UDC}C

\iota_{UDC}C

\eta_{2,H}

1\to 3

\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket

\iota_{UDC}\tilde{C}

\times_{0.49}\LebNum\iota_{UDC}\tilde{C}

\eta_{H,3}

3\to 2

\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket

\Id

\Id

\eta_{3,2}

It only remains to verify that the given
F_{i,j}
are indeed with post-right adjoints. By construction,
\diam\circ\iota_{UDC}\circ C
is post-r.a.e.. By Lemma
6.1
and Lemma
6.2
,
F_{2,1}
is post-r.a.e. too. By a similar reasoning, the functor
\times_{2.01}\circ\diam\circ\iota_{UDC}\circ C
is post-r.a.e. too. But by commutation in (
23
), this functor equals
\diam\circ\iota_{UDC}\circ\tilde{C}
is post-r.a.e. too. Since
\left(\diam,\LebNum\right)
is a qualifying pair of functors by Theorem
2
, by Theorem
5
(iii),
F_{1,3}=\iota_{UDC}\circ\tilde{C}
is post-r.a.e. too. Finally,
F_{3,1}
is trivially post-r.a.e.. Thus the nodes
1,2,3
belong to the same strongly connected component of the entanglement graph, so by Theorem
7
,

\colim\Pressure_{2}=\colim\Pressure_{3}=\colim\Pressure_{1}=\colim\mathcal{L}_{h}\left(\diam\right),

(24)

with the last equality following from Theorem
5
(iv). Equation (
24
) is the functorial statement of Corollary
1
, which we are now ready to prove. As a result we have :

\begin{split}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.2}\sup_{\alpha}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\ln\CovNum\left(\alpha^{(n)}\right)}=\colim\text{ LogLim }h,\quad\mbox{ categorical interpretation }\\
&=\colim\text{ LogLim }\mathcal{L}_{h}\left(\diam\right)=\colim\text{ LogLim }h\Gamma,\quad\mbox{ by Theorem~\ref{thm:lim_qual} },\\
&={\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.2}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\diam(\alpha)\geq\epsilon}\frac{1}{n}\ln\CovNum\left(\alpha^{(n)}\right)},\quad\mbox{ categorical interpretation }\\
&=\colim\text{ LogLim }\Pressure_{2}=\colim\text{ LogLim }\Pressure_{3},\quad\mbox{ Theorem~\ref{thm:entangle} },\\
&={\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.2}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\ln H_{2}(\epsilon,n)}={\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0.2}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\ln H_{3}(\epsilon,n)}.\end{split}

All the limits from (
5
) have been shown above in green. We have utilized their categorical interpretations, namely –
\Pressure_{1}
from
(
14
), and
\Pressure_{2},\Pressure_{3}
from (
16
), to establish their equality via the equality of colimits in (
24
). This proves Corollary
1
. ∎

This completes the description of our results. The rest of the paper contains the proofs to the various category theoretical theorems and lemmas.

7  Proof of the theorems and lemmas

7.1  Proof of Lemma
2.2

The topological definition of the diameter of an open cover
c
is the maximum diameter of any of its components. The diameter of a set is defined to be the maximum possible distance between any two elements of the set. Thus if a set
S
has diameter
r
, then for every
r^{\prime}>r
, it would fit within a disk of diameter
r^{\prime}
. Conversely, one could define the diameter of
S
to be the infimum of all those
r^{\prime}
for which there is a disk of diameter
r^{\prime}
that would contain
S
. Thus one may define the diameter of an open cover
c
as

\diam(c):=\inf\left\{r\;:\;\mbox{ there is an UDC of diameter }r\mbox{ coarser than }c\right\}.

But the collection of UDC-s which are coarser than
c
are precisely the left-slice category
\left[\iota_{UDC}\;;\;c\right]
. Because of the ordering of the numbers
(0,1]
in
\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket
, the operation of taking infimum of a set becomes a colimit. Thus the diameter becomes

\diam(c)=\colim\left[\left[\iota_{UDC}\;;\;c\right]\xrightarrow{\Forget}\left\llbracket\text{UDC}\right\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\Grain}\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket\right].

(25)

This is precisely the pointwise definition of the left Kan extension, so indeed
\diam=\mathcal{L}_{\iota_{UDC}}\left(\Grain\right)
.

The topological definition of the Lebesgue number of an open cover
c
is the supremum of all
\delta>0
such that given any disk
U
of diameter
\delta
, there is a piece of
c
which properly contains
U
. This is equivalent to saying that the open cover
\Free(r)
formed by taking the union of all possible disks of diameter
r
, is finer than
c
. Thus

\LebNum(c)=\sup\left\{r\;:\;\Free(r)\mbox{ is finer than }c\right\}

To say that
\Free(c)
is finer than
c
is to say that there is a morphism
c\to\iota(\Free(r))
. Thus the Lebesgue number can be rewritten as

\LebNum(c):=\lim\left[\left[c\;;\;\iota\Free\right]\xrightarrow{\Forget}\left\llbracket\downarrow_{0+}^{1}\right\rrbracket\right].

(26)

This is precisely the pointwise definition of the right Kan extension. So
\LebNum=\mathcal{R}_{\iota\Free}\left(\Id_{\mathcal{E}}\right)
. This completes the poof of Lemma
2.2
. ∎

7.2  Proof of Theorem
2

We begin with part (i) by showing that :

D=\mathcal{L}_{\iota}\left(G\right)=\mathcal{L}_{\iota G^{r}}\left(\Id_{\mathcal{E}}\right).

(27)

To see why, first note that
D
is a left extension of
\Id_{\mathcal{E}}
along
\iota G^{r}
, namely :

D\iota G^{r}\Leftarrow GG^{r}=\Id_{\mathcal{E}}.

To show that
D
is a left Kan extension, it has to be shown that
D
is minimal. So let
F
be any other left extension of
\Id_{\mathcal{E}}
along
\iota G^{r}
, i.e.,
\Id_{\mathcal{E}}\Rightarrow F\iota G^{r}
. It has to be shown that
D\Rightarrow F
. Then

F\iota=F\iota\Id_{\tilde{X}}\Leftarrow F\iota F^{r}G\Leftarrow\Id_{\mathcal{E}}G=G.

Thus
F
is a left extension of
G
along
\iota
. But since
D=\mathcal{L}_{\iota}\left(G\right)
,
G\Rightarrow F
as claimed.
The following lemma is about such left and right Kan extensions of the same functor pair.

Lemma 7.1
.

Consider a pair of functors
A\xleftarrow{\alpha}B\xrightarrow{\beta}C
such that

1.

C
is a preorder;

2.

there is a full-functor
\gamma:C\to A
such that
\gamma\circ\beta=\alpha
;

3.

the right and left Kan extensions
\mathcal{R}_{\alpha}\left(\beta\right),\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}\left(\beta\right)
exist.

Then
\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}\left(\beta\right)\Rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\alpha}\left(\beta\right)
.

Lemma
7.1
follows from arguments in coend calculus and the author is referred to
(
Maclane2013
, Sec X)
. Lemma
7.1
is satisfied in our case by the substitutions :

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Thus by Lemma
7.1
,
D\Rightarrow L
, satisfying the first requirement to being a qualifying pair.

Now suppose that
\mathcal{E}
is totally ordered. Any object in the comma category
\left[L\;;\;D\right]
is pair
x,y\in ob(X)
such that
L(y)\to D(x)
. By construction

D(x)=\inf E/x,\,E/x:=\left\{e\in\mathcal{E}\;:\;\iota G^{r}(e)\to x\right\},\quad L(y):=\sup y/E,\,y/E:=\left\{e\in\mathcal{E}\;:\;y\to\iota G^{r}(e)\right\}

There is an increasing sequence of points
l_{1}\leftarrow l_{2}\leftarrow l_{3}\leftarrow\ldots
in
y/E
converging to
L(y)
from the left. Similarly, there is a sequence of points
d_{1}\rightarrow d_{2}\rightarrow d_{3}\rightarrow\ldots
in
E/x
converging to
D(x)
from the right. Thus for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

D(x)\leftarrow d_{n}\leftarrow l_{n}\leftarrow L(y),\quad d_{n}\in E/x,\,l_{n}\in y/E.

Then by definition of the sets
E/x
,
y/E
,

x\leftarrow\iota G^{r}(d_{n})\leftarrow\iota G^{r}(l_{n})\leftarrow y.

Thus there is a mapping
\Psi
of
\left[L\;;\;D\right]
into
\left[\Id_{X}\;;\;\Id_{X}\right]
. This makes
(L,D)
a qualifying pair of functors. This completes the proof of Theorem
2
. ∎

This means there is a morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(F^{\prime}i,G^{\prime}i,\eta_{i}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(F^{\prime}i^{\prime},G^{\prime}i^{\prime},\eta_{i^{\prime}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(F^{\prime}\gamma,G^{\prime}\gamma\right)}

so by (
28
), we have the commutation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}i^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi\left(\eta_{i}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi\left(\eta_{i^{\prime}}\right)}

Thus there is indeed a natural transformation from
F^{\prime}
to
G^{\prime}
, with
\Psi(\eta_{i})
being the component of this transformation at any point
i\in ob(I)
. This completes the proof of Theorem
3
. ∎

7.4  Proof of Lemma
4.2

We only prove Claim (i), as the proof for Claim (ii) is analogous.

Consider the functors
R:B\to C,RT:A\to C,RTT^{*}:B\to C
, mapping into
C
. Let their limits be respectively
c_{R},c_{RT},c_{RTT^{*}}
. Thus there is a natural transformation
\eta^{(R)}
such that
\left(c_{R},\eta^{(R)}\right)
is the minimal cone over the functor
R
. Thus
\eta^{(R)}
is a natural transformation from the constant
c_{R}
-valued functor
B\to C
, to
R
. One can define
\eta^{(RT)},\eta^{(RTT^{*})}
analogously. In summary

\eta^{(R)}:c_{R}\Rightarrow R,\quad\eta^{(RT)}:c_{RT}\Rightarrow RT,\quad\eta^{(RTT^{*})}:c_{RTT^{*}}\Rightarrow RTT^{*}.

Any cone over
R
can be restricted to a cone over
RT
. Thus there is a unique morphism
\phi:c_{R}\to c_{RT}
which pulls back the cone
\left(c_{RT},\eta^{(RT)}\right)
into the restriction of the cone
\left(c_{R},\eta^{(R)}\right)
to
RT
, namely :

(29)

Since
T^{*}
is a pre-right adjoint of
T
, there is a natural transformation
\eta:TT^{*}\Rightarrow\Id_{B}
. Thus

(30)

We now prove that
c_{R}
is also the limit for
RTT^{*}
. To see why, consider any cone
c_{RTT^{*}}
over
RTT^{*}
, with connecting morphisms
\eta^{(RTT^{*})}
. By (
30
), this extend to a cone over the functor
R
and we must have a unique morphism
\gamma:c_{RTT^{*}}\to c_{R}
such that the following diagram commutes.

(31)

The diagram in (
31
) reveals that
c_{R}
itself is the apex of a cone over
RTT^{*}
, with connecting morphisms
\eta_{b}^{(RTT^{*})}\psi\phi
. Moreover, the arbitary cone at
c_{RTT^{*}}
factorizes through this cone. Thus
c_{R}
must be the limit of
RTT^{*}
. Since the cone over
RTT^{*}
from
c_{R}
itself factorizes via the cone from
c_{RT}
, we must have
c_{R}=c_{RT}=c_{RTT^{*}}
, as claimed. ∎

7.5  Proof of Theorem
5

Claim (i) follows from the following chain of morphisms in
Z
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\colim mP}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\colim m}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\colim\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(m\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\colim\left(\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(m\right)\circ D\right).}
by Lem
4.1

by (
6
)
by Lem
4.1

(32)

Next Claim (ii) will be proven. Let
\Psi
be the post-right adjoint of
DP
, i.e.,
\Id_{N}\Rightarrow DP\Psi
. Thus

L\Rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(L\right)D\Rightarrow DP\Psi\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(L\right)D

(L,D)
form a qualifying pair of functors, so by Theorem
3

\Id_{X}\Rightarrow P\Psi\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(L\right)D

(33)

Equation (
33
) provides the post-right adjoint of
P
namely
\Psi\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(L\right)D
. This proves Claim (ii). Claim (iii) now follows from Lemma
4.2
(ii).

To prove Claim (iv) we compose both sides of (
33
) with the functor
m
to get :

m\Rightarrow mP\Psi\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(L\right)D

By the minimality of the relation
m\Rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(m\right)D
, we must have

\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(m\right)\Rightarrow mP\Psi\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(L\right)

(34)

In consequence, we get the ordering :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\colim\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(m\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\colim mP\Psi\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(L\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\colim mP.}
by (
34
)
Lem
4.1

(35)

Claim(v) follows from (
32
), (
35
), and the fact that
Z
is a preorder. ∎

7.6  Proof of Corollary
6

Note that the sequence
x_{n}
can be interpreted as the image of a functor
Q:\left\{\mathbb{N}\right\}\to X
. Consider the left Kan extension of
Q
along the inclusion
\iota_{+}:\left\{\mathbb{N}\right\}\to\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket
, given by

\bar{Q}:=\mathcal{L}_{Q}\left(\iota_{+}\right):\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\to X,\quad\bar{Q}(n):=\coprod_{j=1}^{n}x_{n}.

Also by assumption, there is a functor
R:\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\to\mathcal{E}
such that the following commutation holds :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\{\mathbb{N}\right\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}

Therefore, we have

R\iota_{+}=DQ\Rightarrow D\bar{Q}\iota_{+}.

Since
\iota_{+}:\left\{\mathbb{N}\right\}\to\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket
is bijective on objects, a natural transformation
R\iota_{+}\Rightarrow D\bar{Q}\iota_{+}
implies a natural transformation
R\Rightarrow D\bar{Q}
. Then since
R
has a post right adjoint, say
\Psi:\mathcal{E}\to\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket
, we have

\Id_{\mathcal{E}}\Rightarrow R\Psi\Rightarrow D\bar{Q}\Psi.

Thus
\Psi
is a post right adjoint to
D\bar{Q}
too. Thus, the functor
\bar{Q}:\left\llbracket\mathbb{N}\uparrow\right\rrbracket\to X
satisfies the criterion on the functor
P:N\to X
from Claim (i). Thus we must have

\colim\mathcal{L}_{D}\left(m\right)\to\colim m\bar{Q}=\limsup_{n\to\infty}m(x_{n}),

proving Corollary
6
.

7.7  Proof of Theorem
7

Both claims of the theorem will be proved if it can be shown that for each edge
i\to j
in
\mathcal{G}
,
\lim F_{i}\to\lim F_{j}
. Since
S
is cocomplete, for any category
A
, the operation of taking colimits is a functor

\lim:S^{A}\to S

Now take
A=C_{i}
. Then
F_{i}
and
F_{j}\circ F_{i,j}
are two objects in the functor category
S^{C_{i}}
, with
\eta_{i,j}
being a morphism between them. Thus by the functorial property of
\colim
, we have
\colim F_{i}\to\colim F_{j}\circ F_{i,j}
. By Lemma
4.1
,
\colim F_{j}\circ F_{i,j}\to\colim F_{j}
and thus
\colim F_{i}\to\colim F_{j}
. ∎

7.8  Proof of Lemma
6.1

Suppose that in the composite diagram
A\xrightarrow{F}B\xrightarrow{G}C
, both
F
and
G
have post right adjoints
F^{*}
and
G^{*}
respectively. Then note that

\Id_{C}\Rightarrow GG^{*}=G\Id_{B}G^{*}\Rightarrow GFF^{*}G^{*},

making
F^{*}G^{*}
a post right adjoint of
GF
. ∎
